       m(z)-\partial_z\log\det(\mathrm I+D M_k(z))$$ have jointly the same law as the corresponding imaginary parts of $m(z)$. The identity is unconditional. In particular it applies to piecewise translations and to arbitrary measurable root-excluded selections of whether to perform them.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-potential-switch"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-potential-switch</summary>

*Proof.* First suppose the selections depend on fixed-radius potential balls through continuity sets, and the maps are piecewise translations with only null sets of discontinuities. At finite $n$, use these same selections and maps when the $k$ sampled balls are disjoint trees; otherwise make no change. Conditional on the graph, the sampling data, and all potentials except the $k$ central ones, every selection is fixed and every central potential remains independent and uniform. Applying the selected maps therefore preserves the joint finite-graph law. The probability of ball or separation failure is $O_k(n^{-1})$ for the fixed radii.

<a id="eq:finite-potential-trace-identity"></a>
Write $H'=H+W_{n,k}D W_{n,k}^{\mathsf T}$ on this event, and let $x_i'$ be its microscopic eigenvalues. The determinant identity and differentiation in the microscopic variable give exactly $$\label{eq:finite-potential-trace-identity}
 \sum_i\frac1{x_i'-z}
   =\sum_i\frac1{x_i-z}
                  -\partial_z\log\det(\mathrm I+D M_{n,k}(z)).$$ Indeed the determinant ratio is $\det(H'-E-z/n)/\det(H-E-z/n)$; its logarithmic derivative is $-n^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}G'(E+z/n)+n^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}G(E+z/n)$. This also verifies the sign and the absence of any further factor of $n$ in <a href="#eq:finite-potential-trace-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-potential-trace-identity">[eq:finite-potential-trace-identity]</a>.

Take imaginary parts. The scalar sums converge by the tail argument preceding <a href="#eq:limiting-poisson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:limiting-poisson">[eq:limiting-poisson]</a>; the compression functions and their derivatives converge locally uniformly. On the coordinates where $D_\alpha\ne0$, write the logarithmic derivative as <a id="eq:switch-log-derivative"></a>$$\label{eq:switch-log-derivative}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\bigl((D^{-1}+M_k(z))^{-1}M_k'(z)\bigr),$$ using the corresponding compression. Positive definiteness of $\Im M_k(z)$ makes $D^{-1}+M_k(z)$ invertible. Zero coordinates can simply be removed. Equivalently, the determinant of $\mathrm I+D M_k$ is nonzero, and its logarithmic derivative has no choice-of-branch ambiguity. The continuous mapping theorem now passes <a href="#eq:finite-potential-trace-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-potential-trace-identity">[eq:finite-potential-trace-identity]</a> to the limit, jointly in any finite list of arguments, and law preservation gives the claimed identity for these selections and maps.

For completeness, no continuity assumption on a fixed measure-preserving map is needed in the stated result. A bounded measurable $T_\alpha$ can, for convergence purposes, be approximated in probability under the uniform root law by continuous functions (or by functions with finitely many interval discontinuities). This follows from approximation of measurable sets in Lebesgue measure by finite unions of intervals, applied to a finite binning of its range. The approximants need not preserve measure: the finite-graph law-preservation identity always uses $T_\alpha$ itself. They only establish joint convergence of $T_\alpha(V_\alpha)$ with the resolvents and environment data. The approximation probabilities are the same at every $n$ and in the limit because the root marginals are uniform. This proves the fixed-radius assertion for every measurable measure-preserving map.

Finally, approximate each general $B_\alpha$ in probability by events in finitely many root-excluded coordinates. Such approximation follows from conditional expectations on increasing finite-radius sigma-fields. Each finite-coordinate event can in turn be approximated by a continuity set for its finite product uniform law. All these approximations exclude the root potential. For fixed $k$, the probability that any of the selected maps differs from its approximation tends to zero. On their agreement event, <a href="#eq:potential-switch-transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:potential-switch-transform">[eq:potential-switch-transform]</a> is exactly unchanged; therefore its finite-dimensional law converges in the approximation. The preceding identity passes to the limit. Independent branch orderings can be included among the sampling data when an event uses ordered coordinates. ◻

</details>

All limits in this section use a fixed number of sampled sites. The next section increases that number inside the already constructed limiting law, using Proposition <a href="#prop:potential-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:potential-switch">19</a> at each finite value.

<a id="sec:insertion"></a>
### Gaussian insertion by potential permutations

Fix a microscopic subsequential law $\nu=\sum_j\delta_{x_j}$ from Sections <a href="#sec:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:entropy">4</a>–<a href="#sec:resolvents" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:resolvents">6</a>. We shall approximate its law by eigenvalues of a finite diagonal matrix plus Gaussian noise. The number of potential changes will grow only inside this already constructed limiting probability space. Thus the finite-list Gaussian mark theorem is sufficient; no assertion about a growing list of finite-graph vertices is needed.

The mechanism is to make many small, measure-preserving potential changes. The potential-switch identity will represent their effect, after truncating the spectral sum, by signed rank-one perturbations of a diagonal matrix of microscopic positions. Conditional on those positions and the sampled environments, the Gaussian marks make the perturbation’s mean scalar and its covariance that of a GOE matrix. We will compare it with Gaussian noise after controlling its spectral tails. The scalar mean need not vanish, so it will remain in the comparison matrix.

The matrix estimates below adapt the Gaussian insertion argument of <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[bu](#ref-bu), Sections 5.3–5.5]. We give their proofs because the coefficients here come from potential permutations, and their size and cancellation must be established for the disordered model.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### Potential permutations and their coefficients

We use $r$ sampled roots and potential increments of size comparable to $\theta$, chosen so that their total variance has scale $T=r\theta^2$. Two spectral cutoffs serve different purposes. A large outer cutoff $L$ makes the switch determinant a finite matrix expression, with a negligible tail even though the determinant has up to $r$ coordinates. We will then remove the positions outside the much smaller window $[-K,K]$. The remaining matrix has order $K$, small enough for the Gaussian replacement error to vanish.

<a id="eq:sw-parameters"></a>
Decrease the deterministic discrepancy exponent in Lemma <a href="#lem:discrepancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:discrepancy">16</a> if necessary so that $0<\chi<1/4$, and fix the following scales: $$\label{eq:sw-parameters}
 0<\tau<\chi/100000,\qquad
 T=r^\tau,\quad K=r^{1/100},\quad L=r^{100/\chi},\qquad
 \theta=(T/r)^{1/2}.$$ All limits in this section have $r\to\infty$. For clarity, the almost-sure consequences used later will be taken along the deterministic sequence $r=2^j$; any faster increasing integer sequence would also do.

At each independently sampled tree root $\alpha$, write $$m_\alpha^*(v)=(v-E-\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^d g_{\alpha i})^{-1},\qquad
 a_\alpha(v)=\Re m_\alpha^*(v),\qquad
 c_\alpha(v)=\frac{\Im m_\alpha^*(v)}{\pi\rho}.$$ Here $v\in[-w,w]$ is the root potential and the incoming cavity values depend only on the environment away from that root. They are finite with positive imaginary parts almost surely. We can therefore choose a fixed event $\mathcal A$, measurable without the root potential, of positive probability, on which $$|E+\textstyle\sum_i\Re g_{\alpha i}|\le C_0,\qquad
 c_0\le\textstyle\sum_i\Im g_{\alpha i}\le C_0$$ for fixed $c_0,C_0>0$. On $\mathcal A$, the functions $a_\alpha,c_\alpha$ are uniformly bounded and Lipschitz on $[-w,w]$, and $c_\alpha$ is bounded below by a positive constant. These statements follow directly by differentiating $(v-A-iB)^{-1}$ on the displayed compact range of $A,B,v$.

Choose an integer $N_r$ such that $\ell_r=w/N_r\asymp\theta$, and partition $[-w,w]$ into $N_r$ intervals of length $2\ell_r$. On $\mathcal A$, interchange the two halves of each interval by translation. Off $\mathcal A$, leave the potential unchanged. This is a measure-preserving involution conditionally on the root-excluded information. Denote its increment at the first $r$ sampled roots by $D_\alpha$; at an active root, $D_\alpha=\pm\ell_r$. Proposition <a href="#prop:potential-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:potential-switch">19</a> applies to these choices.

<a id="eq:insertion-coefficients"></a>
Evaluate $a_\alpha,c_\alpha$ at the original root potential, and define <a id="eq:sw-coefficient-bounds"></a>$$\label{eq:sw-coefficient-bounds}
 \beta_\alpha=\frac{c_\alpha D_\alpha}{1+a_\alpha D_\alpha},\qquad
 \beta=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_r),\qquad
 s_1=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\beta,\quad s_2=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\beta^2.$$ Inactive coordinates have $\beta_\alpha=0$. For all large $r$, the denominators are bounded away from zero, and $$\label{eq:insertion-coefficients}
 \|\beta\|\le C\theta,\qquad
 cT\le s_2\le CT,\qquad |s_1|\le CT$$ with probability tending to one. The first and upper second bounds are deterministic. The lower second bound follows from the positive proportion of active roots, since $|\beta_\alpha|\asymp\ell_r$ there. For the last bound, pairing the two halves of each interval gives $$|\mathbb E\beta_\alpha|\le C\ell_r^2,
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\beta_\alpha)\le C\ell_r^2.$$ Indeed the contributions $\ell_r c(v)$ and $-\ell_r c(v+\ell_r)$ cancel to order $\ell_r^2$, and the factor $(1+aD)^{-1}$ differs from one by $O(\ell_r)$. Independence of the root environments now gives $|\mathbb Es_1|\le CT$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}s_1\le CT$. Chebyshev’s inequality makes the failure probability $O(T^{-1})$; the active-site count has exponentially small failure probability. Along $r=2^j$ these failures are summable, so <a href="#eq:insertion-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:insertion-coefficients">[eq:insertion-coefficients]</a> holds eventually almost surely.

Let $\mathcal E$ be the sigma field generated by $\nu$ and all sampled tree environments, including their root potentials. We shall condition on almost every realization of $\mathcal E$ for which <a href="#eq:insertion-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:insertion-coefficients">[eq:insertion-coefficients]</a>, Lemma <a href="#lem:discrepancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:discrepancy">16</a>, and Proposition <a href="#prop:transform-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transform-regularity">18</a> hold eventually. After this conditioning, all coefficients and positions are deterministic; the standardized Gaussian marks remain independent standard Gaussian variables.

Put $I=\{j:|x_j|\le K\}$, $m_I=|I|$, and $D_I=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(x_j:j\in I)$. In this section a *raw GOE* matrix has independent upper-triangular entries of variance one off the diagonal and two on the diagonal, and centered Gaussian entries throughout.

<a id="stmt-prop-gaussian-insertion"></a>
<div id="prop:gaussian-insertion" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:insertion-matrix"></a>
**Proposition 20** (Gaussian insertion). *On the preceding extension of any microscopic subsequential law, let $\mathcal W_r$ be the upper-left $m_I$-dimensional block of an infinite raw GOE array independent of $\mathcal E$, and set $$\label{eq:insertion-matrix}
 H_r=D_I+s_1\mathrm I_{m_I}+\sqrt{s_2}\,\mathcal W_r.$$ Then, along $r=2^j$, the raw eigenvalue counting measures $$\nu^{(r)}=\sum_{j=1}^{m_I}\delta_{\lambda_j(H_r)}
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad\nu$$ converge in law for the local vague topology, without conditioning. Moreover $m_I=2\rho K+O(K^{1-\chi})$ almost surely, and the coefficient bounds <a href="#eq:insertion-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:insertion-coefficients">[eq:insertion-coefficients]</a> hold eventually almost surely.*

</div>

The proof follows the two cutoffs just described. First, truncation at $L$ turns the potential-switch determinant into the resolvent trace of $D_x+\mathcal G\beta\mathcal G^{\mathsf T}$, where $D_x$ lists the retained positions and $\mathcal G$ has independent standard Gaussian entries. Weighted resolvent estimates then remove the positions outside $[-K,K]$. Finally, a second-order replacement of the independent rank-one summands gives the scalar shift $s_1$ and GOE variance $s_2$ in <a href="#eq:insertion-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:insertion-matrix">[eq:insertion-matrix]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### The first cutoff and the finite matrix

<a id="eq:sw-tail-sums"></a>
The discrepancy estimate and partial summation give $$\label{eq:sw-tail-sums}
 \nu([-R,R])=O(R),\qquad
 \left|\mathop{\mathrm{PV}}\sum_{|x_j|>R}\frac1{x_j}\right|=O(R^{-\chi}),\qquad
 \sum_{|x_j|>R}\frac1{x_j^2}=O(R^{-1}).$$ These bounds hold for all sufficiently large $R$, with constants depending on $\nu$. Write $$M_r(z)=A_r+C_r^{1/2}
       \left(\mathop{\mathrm{PV}}\sum_j\frac{g_jg_j^\top}{x_j-z}\right)C_r^{1/2},
 \quad A_r=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(a_\alpha),\quad C_r=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(c_\alpha),$$ using Proposition <a href="#prop:marked-resolvent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:marked-resolvent">17</a>; the $g_j$’s are independent standard Gaussian vectors of dimension $r$. We restrict inverse matrices below to active coordinates, whose number is at most $r$. All norm estimates through the first-cut comparison concern these active compressions. On them $C_r$ is bounded above and below uniformly; inactive coordinates contribute the identity to the switch determinant.

<a id="eq:insertion-first-tail"></a>
For a fixed finite list of $z$’s in $\mathbb H$, let $M_{r,L}$ be the same expression restricted to $|x_j|\le L$. Conditional on $\mathcal E$, $$\label{eq:insertion-first-tail}
 \|M_r-M_{r,L}\|+\|M_r'-M_{r,L}'\|
       =O_{\mathbb P}(r^2L^{-\chi}),\qquad
 \|M_r'\|=O_{\mathbb P}(r^2).$$ For the means use <a href="#eq:sw-tail-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-tail-sums">[eq:sw-tail-sums]</a>. Each centered entry of the tail has variance $O(L^{-1})$, and summing squared entries proves the bound in Hilbert–Schmidt norm; the derivative has an even smaller tail. The derivative bound for the full matrix follows from $\sum_j|x_j-z|^{-2}<\infty$, first moments of Gaussian squares, and the Hilbert–Schmidt or trace bound for its absolute summands.

With probability tending to one, both $\Im M_r(z)$ and $\Im M_{r,L}(z)$ are at least $r^{-5}\mathrm I$ on active coordinates. To see this, retain only points $|x_j|\le r^4$. There are $2\rho r^4+O(r^{4(1-\chi)})$ such points; each Poisson weight is at least $c_zr^{-8}$. Their empirical Gaussian covariance tends in operator norm to the identity: the expected squared Hilbert–Schmidt error is $O(r^2/r^4)=O(r^{-2})$. The uniform lower bound on $C_r$ then gives a lower bound of order $r^{-4}$, and hence the claimed weaker bound. The same retained points occur in the cutoff because $L>r^4$.

The potential increment matrix $D=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(D_\alpha)$ is real and invertible on active coordinates. Thus $$\|(D^{-1}+M_r)^{-1}\|+
 \|(D^{-1}+M_{r,L})^{-1}\|\le2r^5$$ on the preceding event. The inverse identity and <a href="#eq:insertion-first-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:insertion-first-tail">[eq:insertion-first-tail]</a> therefore show that replacing $M_r$ by $M_{r,L}$ in $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}((D^{-1}+M_r)^{-1}M_r')$ costs $o_{\mathbb P}(1)$. Explicitly, after the trace and derivative are bounded, the inverse difference multiplies the tail error by at most $O_{\mathbb P}(r^{13})$, and $r^{15}L^{-\chi}=r^{-85}\to0$. The scalar principal-value transform $m$ may also be cut at $L$ with an error tending to zero by <a href="#eq:sw-tail-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-tail-sums">[eq:sw-tail-sums]</a>.

<a id="eq:sw-finite-matrix"></a>
Let $D_x=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(x_j:|x_j|\le L)$, and let $\mathcal G$ have rows indexed by these points and $r$ independent standard Gaussian columns. Determinant interchange gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \det(\mathrm I+DM_{r,L}(z))
 &=\det(\mathrm I+DA_r)\,
   \det\bigl(\mathrm I+\beta\mathcal G^\top(D_x-z)^{-1}\mathcal G\bigr)\\
 &=\det(\mathrm I+DA_r)\,
   \frac{\det(D_x+\mathcal G\beta\mathcal G^\top-z)}{\det(D_x-z)}.
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, if $$\mathcal S_r(z)=m(z)-\partial_z\log\det(\mathrm I+DM_r(z)),
 \qquad A'=\mathcal G\beta\mathcal G^\top,$$ then $$\label{eq:sw-finite-matrix}
 \mathcal S_r(z)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(D_x+A'-z)^{-1}+o_{\mathbb P}(1).$$ Only the logarithmic derivative is used, so no choice of a logarithm branch is required. For every fixed $r$, the joint unconditional law of the imaginary parts of $\mathcal S_r$ is that of $m$, by Proposition <a href="#prop:potential-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:potential-switch">19</a>.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Removing the outer positions

The finite matrix in <a href="#eq:sw-finite-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-finite-matrix">[eq:sw-finite-matrix]</a> still has order $L$. We next compare its resolvent trace with that of the inner block indexed by $I$. This will leave a matrix of order $K$, small enough for the Gaussian replacement.

Let $F=\{j:K<|x_j|\le L\}$, so that the indices of $D_x$ split into $I,F$. For a fixed $z\in\mathbb H$, define the separate block resolvents $$G_F=(D_F+A'_{FF}-z)^{-1},\qquad
 G_I=(D_I+A'_{II}-z)^{-1}.$$ The upper-left block of the full resolvent is $(G_I^{-1}-\Sigma)^{-1}$, where $$\Sigma=A'_{IF}G_FA'_{FI}.$$ Thus we must control both the direct far-block trace and the effect of this Schur correction on the inner resolvent.

<a id="eq:sw-weight-bounds"></a>
The diagonal positions give a natural weight on the far block, where $|x_j|>K$. On the inner block we regularize the same weight at the larger height $h_1=T^2$, where the perturbation tends to zero in the weighted norm used below. Set <a id="eq:sw-weights"></a>$$\label{eq:sw-weights}
 h_1=T^2,\qquad U_I=(|D_I|+h_1)^{-1/2},\qquad
 U_F=|D_F|^{-1/2}.$$ Partial summation using <a href="#eq:sw-tail-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-tail-sums">[eq:sw-tail-sums]</a> gives $$\label{eq:sw-weight-bounds}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}U_I^2+\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}U_F^2=O(\log r),\qquad
 \|U_I^2\|\le h_1^{-1},\quad \|U_F^2\|\le K^{-1}.$$ For example, the inner sum is bounded by integrating the linear count bound against $(|x|+h_1)^{-1}$; the far sum is treated in the same way between $K$ and $L$. Both endpoints are fixed powers of $r$. In the following estimates, $\operatorname{polylog}(r)$ denotes a fixed power of $\log r$, whose exponent may increase from one occurrence to the next.

We will show that $G_F$ is close to $D_F^{-1}$ in these weights, that $\|U_I\Sigma U_I\|=o_{\mathbb P}(T^{-5})$, and that $\|U_I^{-1}G_I(z)U_I^{-1}\|=O_{\mathbb P}(T^2)$. The last two estimates make the inner resolvent change small even after the two inverse factors in the resolvent identity. The logarithmic trace bounds above then turn weighted norm control into trace control. We begin with the Gaussian block estimates that supply these bounds.

<a id="stmt-sw-weighted-blocks"></a>
<div id="sw:weighted-blocks" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sw-bernstein"></a>
<a id="eq:sw-cross-block"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Weighted block estimates). *For $\mathcal A=I,F$, put $a_I=h_1$, $a_F=K$. With probability tending to one, $$\label{eq:sw-bernstein}
 \left\|U_{\mathcal A}
       (A'_{\mathcal A\mathcal A}-s_1\mathrm I)
       U_{\mathcal A}\right\|
 \le \operatorname{polylog}(r)
       \left(\sqrt{T/a_{\mathcal A}}+\theta\right).$$ Moreover, for $P_0=U_IA'_{IF}U_F$, $$\label{eq:sw-cross-block}
 \|P_0\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le T\operatorname{polylog}(r)$$ with probability tending to one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sw-weighted-blocks"><summary>Proof of stmt-sw-weighted-blocks</summary>

<a id="eq:sw-fourth-moment"></a>
*Proof.* The centered matrix in <a href="#eq:sw-bernstein" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-bernstein">[eq:sw-bernstein]</a> is a sum of independent self-adjoint matrices $$\sum_{\alpha=1}^{r}\beta_{\alpha\alpha}
       (v_\alpha v_\alpha^\top-P),\qquad P=U_{\mathcal A}^2,$$ where $v_\alpha$ is Gaussian of covariance $P$. Its coefficients may have either sign. Gaussian fourth moments give $$\label{eq:sw-fourth-moment}
 \mathbb E[(v_\alpha v_\alpha^\top)^2]
   =(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}P)P+2P^2.$$ Truncate each original Gaussian column on the event that all its entries have absolute value at most $\log r$, and center the truncated summand. There are only polynomially many entries, so this alters the matrices with superpolynomially small probability. Gaussian tails also make the resulting centering correction superpolynomially small.

Each truncated summand has norm at most $$C\|\beta\|(\log r)^2\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}P
 \le \theta\operatorname{polylog}(r).$$ If $X$ is a self-adjoint random matrix, then $\mathbb E[(X-\mathbb EX)^2]=\mathbb E[X^2]-(\mathbb EX)^2\le\mathbb E[X^2]$. Also truncating $v_\alpha v_\alpha^\top$ by an event can only decrease the expectation of its square in positive semidefinite order. Thus <a href="#eq:sw-fourth-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-fourth-moment">[eq:sw-fourth-moment]</a>, including for negative $\beta_{\alpha\alpha}$, bounds the matrix variance parameter by $$C s_2(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}P)\|P\|
 \le \frac{T}{a_{\mathcal A}}\operatorname{polylog}(r).$$ The bounded self-adjoint matrix Bernstein inequality <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Tropp2012](#ref-Tropp2012), Theorem 1.4], applied to both signs, gives, for a sum of independent centered self-adjoint matrices of norm at most $b$ and variance parameter $\sigma_{\rm mat}^2$, a two-sided tail bounded by $$2|\mathcal A|\exp\left(
 -\frac{c t^2}{\sigma_{\rm mat}^2+bt}\right).$$ Its dimension factor is polynomial in $r$. Taking a sufficiently large power of $\log r$ in $t$ proves <a href="#eq:sw-bernstein" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-bernstein">[eq:sw-bernstein]</a>.

For the cross block, independence of distinct Gaussian rows and direct second-moment calculation give $$\mathbb E\|P_0\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =s_2(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}U_I^2)(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}U_F^2).$$ Equation <a href="#eq:sw-cross-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-cross-block">[eq:sw-cross-block]</a> follows from <a href="#eq:sw-weight-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-weight-bounds">[eq:sw-weight-bounds]</a> and Markov’s inequality, with one additional logarithmic factor. ◻

</details>

We first apply the weighted block estimates to the far resolvent $G_F$ and the Schur correction $\Sigma$, keeping $z\in\mathbb H$ fixed.

<a id="stmt-sw-schur"></a>
<div id="sw:schur" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sw-far-resolvent"></a>
<a id="eq:sw-schur-small"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Far block and Schur correction). *Let $$\delta_F=\operatorname{polylog}(r)
       \left(\frac{1+T}{K}+\sqrt{\frac TK}+\theta\right).$$ Then $$\label{eq:sw-far-resolvent}
 \|U_F^{-1}(G_F-D_F^{-1})U_F^{-1}\|\le\delta_F=o(1)$$ with probability tending to one, and $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}G_F=o_{\mathbb P}(1)$. For the Schur correction $\Sigma$, $$\label{eq:sw-schur-small}
 \|U_I\Sigma U_I\|=o_{\mathbb P}(T^{-5}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sw-schur"><summary>Proof of stmt-sw-schur</summary>

*Proof.* Conjugating the matrix to be inverted by $U_F$ gives $$U_F(D_F+A'_{FF}-z)U_F
 =\operatorname{sign}(D_F)+U_F(A'_{FF}-z)U_F.$$ The second term has norm at most $\delta_F$, by Lemma <a href="#sw:weighted-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sw:weighted-blocks">21</a>, the bound $|s_1|=O(T)$, and <a href="#eq:sw-weight-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-weight-bounds">[eq:sw-weight-bounds]</a>. Since $\delta_F=o(1)$, a Neumann expansion proves <a href="#eq:sw-far-resolvent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-far-resolvent">[eq:sw-far-resolvent]</a>. In particular, $Q_F=U_F^{-1}G_FU_F^{-1}$ has bounded norm. By <a href="#eq:sw-tail-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-tail-sums">[eq:sw-tail-sums]</a>, $$\left|\sum_{j\in F}\frac1{x_j}\right|=O(K^{-\chi}),
 \qquad
 |\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(G_F-D_F^{-1})|
 \le\delta_F\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}U_F^2=o(1).$$ This proves the assertion about the far trace.

<a id="eq:sw-schur-replacement"></a>
For the Schur correction, replacing $G_F$ by $D_F^{-1}$ costs at most $$\label{eq:sw-schur-replacement}
 \|P_0\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\delta_F
 \le T\operatorname{polylog}(r)\delta_F$$ in the norm weighted on both sides by $U_I$. It remains to estimate $A'_{IF}D_F^{-1}A'_{FI}$. Condition further on the inner Gaussian row block $\mathcal G_I$, and set $$Q=\mathcal G_I\beta^2\mathcal G_I^\top.$$ The columns of $A'_{IF}$, indexed by $j\in F$, are then independent centered Gaussian vectors $a_j$ with covariance $Q$. Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\left[\sum_{j\in F}\frac{a_ja_j^\top}{x_j}
       \,\middle|\,\mathcal G_I,\mathcal E\right]
 &=Q\sum_{j\in F}\frac1{x_j},\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\left(\sum_{j\in F}\frac{(a_j)_a(a_j)_b}{x_j}
       \,\middle|\,\mathcal G_I,\mathcal E\right)
 &=(Q_{aa}Q_{bb}+Q_{ab}^2)
       \sum_{j\in F}\frac1{x_j^2}.
\end{aligned}$$ Also $\mathbb E[Q_{aa}^2\mid\mathcal E]
 =s_2^2+2\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\beta^4=O(T^2)$. Since $Q$ is positive semidefinite, $|Q_{ab}|^2\le Q_{aa}Q_{bb}$; Cauchy–Schwarz bounds the remaining second moments by $O(T^2)$. Multiplying entry $a,b$ by $(U_I)_{aa}(U_I)_{bb}$, summing its second moment over $a,b$, and using <a href="#eq:sw-tail-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-tail-sums">[eq:sw-tail-sums]</a> and <a href="#eq:sw-weight-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-weight-bounds">[eq:sw-weight-bounds]</a>, we obtain $$\mathbb E\left\|
 U_IA'_{IF}D_F^{-1}A'_{FI}U_I
 \right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le C T^2(\log r)^2(K^{-2\chi}+K^{-1}).$$ Markov’s inequality therefore bounds this norm by $T\operatorname{polylog}(r)(K^{-\chi}+K^{-1/2})$ with probability tending to one.

Combining this estimate with <a href="#eq:sw-schur-replacement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-schur-replacement">[eq:sw-schur-replacement]</a> gives $$\|U_I\Sigma U_I\|
 \le \operatorname{polylog}(r)\,O\left(
 TK^{-\chi}+TK^{-1/2}+\frac{T(1+T)}K
       +T\sqrt{T/K}+T\theta\right)$$ with probability tending to one. After multiplication by $T^5$, all terms tend to zero by a fixed power of $r$. For example, the three potentially slow terms have exponents $$6\tau-\frac{\chi}{100},\qquad
 \frac{13}{2}\tau-\frac1{200},\qquad
 \frac{13}{2}\tau-\frac12,$$ respectively. They are negative under <a href="#eq:sw-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-parameters">[eq:sw-parameters]</a>; the terms $T^6K^{-1/2}$ and $T^6(1+T)/K$ have still ample margins. This proves <a href="#eq:sw-schur-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-schur-small">[eq:sw-schur-small]</a>. ◻

</details>

We now convert the small weighted Schur correction into a small trace error. The fixed-height inner resolvent cannot be estimated directly from <a href="#eq:sw-bernstein" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-bernstein">[eq:sw-bernstein]</a>; instead we first work at the larger height $h_1=T^2$.

<a id="stmt-sw-trace-cut"></a>
<div id="sw:trace-cut" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sw-inner-resolvent"></a>
<a id="eq:sw-second-cut"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Inner resolvent and trace cut). *For every fixed $z\in\mathbb H$, $$\label{eq:sw-inner-resolvent}
 \|U_I^{-1}G_I(z)U_I^{-1}\|=O_{\mathbb P}(h_1),$$ and $$\label{eq:sw-second-cut}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(D_x+A'-z)^{-1}
 =\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(D_I+A'_{II}-z)^{-1}+o_{\mathbb P}(1).$$ The same estimates hold jointly for a fixed finite list of $z$’s.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sw-trace-cut"><summary>Proof of stmt-sw-trace-cut</summary>

*Proof.* Every diagonal entry of $U_I(D_I-ih_1)U_I$ has modulus at least $1/\sqrt2$. The added term has norm at most $$\|U_IA'_{II}U_I\|
 \le C T/h_1+\operatorname{polylog}(r)
       \left(\sqrt{T/h_1}+\theta\right)=o(1)$$ with probability tending to one. Hence $$\|U_I^{-1}G_I(ih_1)U_I^{-1}\|\le C.$$ The identity $G_I(ih_1)^*G_I(ih_1)=h_1^{-1}\Im G_I(ih_1)$ now gives $$\|G_I(ih_1)U_I^{-1}\|+\|U_I^{-1}G_I(ih_1)\|
 \le C h_1^{-1/2}.$$ Alternatively the same mixed-weight estimates follow from the preceding weighted inverse and $\|U_I\|\le h_1^{-1/2}$. Use the exact twice-iterated resolvent identity $$\begin{aligned}
 G_I(z)
 ={}&G_I(ih_1)+(z-ih_1)G_I(ih_1)^2\\
 &+(z-ih_1)^2G_I(ih_1)G_I(z)G_I(ih_1).
\end{aligned}$$ The unweighted middle resolvent in the last term has norm at most $(\Im z)^{-1}$. Conjugating the identity by $U_I^{-1}$, the three terms are bounded by $$C,\qquad C|z-ih_1|/h_1,\qquad
 C|z-ih_1|^2/(h_1\Im z),$$ respectively. This proves <a href="#eq:sw-inner-resolvent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-inner-resolvent">[eq:sw-inner-resolvent]</a>.

The Schur complement formula for the full inverse has upper-left block $\widetilde G_I=(G_I^{-1}-\Sigma)^{-1}$. Set $R_I=U_I^{-1}G_IU_I^{-1}$ and $S_I=U_I\Sigma U_I$. Then $$U_I^{-1}\widetilde G_IU_I^{-1}
 =(R_I^{-1}-S_I)^{-1}.$$ By <a href="#eq:sw-inner-resolvent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-inner-resolvent">[eq:sw-inner-resolvent]</a> and <a href="#eq:sw-schur-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-schur-small">[eq:sw-schur-small]</a>, a Neumann expansion gives $$\|U_I^{-1}\widetilde G_IU_I^{-1}\|=O_{\mathbb P}(h_1),\qquad
 \|U_I^{-1}(\widetilde G_I-G_I)U_I^{-1}\|
 \le O_{\mathbb P}(h_1^2)o_{\mathbb P}(T^{-5})=o_{\mathbb P}(T^{-1}).$$ Multiplying the last bound by $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}U_I^2=O(\log r)$ shows that their traces differ by $o_{\mathbb P}(1)$.

The full block-inverse trace is $$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\widetilde G_I+\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}G_F+
 \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\bigl(\widetilde G_IA'_{IF}G_F^2A'_{FI}\bigr).$$ The far trace is $o_{\mathbb P}(1)$ by Lemma <a href="#sw:schur" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sw:schur">22</a>. For the last term recall $Q_F=U_F^{-1}G_FU_F^{-1}$. It has bounded norm, and $$U_F^{-1}G_F^2U_F^{-1}=Q_FU_F^2Q_F,\qquad
 \|Q_FU_F^2Q_F\|=O_{\mathbb P}(K^{-1}).$$ No positivity is asserted for this complex square. The ordinary operator-norm and Hilbert–Schmidt trace inequality instead gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \left|\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\bigl(\widetilde G_IA'_{IF}G_F^2A'_{FI}\bigr)\right|
 &\le
 \|U_I^{-1}\widetilde G_IU_I^{-1}\|\,
 \|P_0\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\,
 \|Q_FU_F^2Q_F\|\\
 &\le \operatorname{polylog}(r)\,O_{\mathbb P}(T^3/K)=o_{\mathbb P}(1).
\end{aligned}$$ This proves <a href="#eq:sw-second-cut" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-second-cut">[eq:sw-second-cut]</a>. All estimates used the same Gaussian matrices, so taking a finite union gives the joint assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Gaussian replacement and convergence of point measures

We have reduced the switched trace to an inner matrix of size $$m_I=2\rho K+O(K^{1-\chi})\asymp K.$$ Its perturbation is a sum of $r$ independent small rank-one matrices. Only at this reduced size do we replace that sum by GOE noise.

<a id="stmt-sw-lindeberg"></a>
<div id="sw:lindeberg" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Gaussian replacement). *Conditional on almost every realization of $\mathcal E$, bounded smooth tests with bounded derivatives of finitely many trace values have expectation difference $o(1)$ between $$D_I+A'_{II}
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad
 H_r=D_I+s_1\mathrm I_{m_I}+\sqrt{s_2}\,\mathcal W.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sw-lindeberg"><summary>Proof of stmt-sw-lindeberg</summary>

*Proof.* Write $$A'_{II}-s_1\mathrm I_{m_I}
 =\sum_{\alpha=1}^{r}
 \beta_{\alpha\alpha}(g_\alpha g_\alpha^\top-\mathrm I_{m_I}),$$ where the $g_\alpha$ are independent standard $m_I$-dimensional Gaussian vectors. The covariance identity $$\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(g_ag_b,g_cg_d)
 =\delta_{ac}\delta_{bd}+\delta_{ad}\delta_{bc}$$ shows that $gg^\top-\mathrm I$ and a raw GOE matrix have the same entry covariances. Their means are both zero. Thus replace the summands one at a time by $\beta_{\alpha\alpha}\mathcal W_\alpha$, with independent raw GOE matrices $\mathcal W_\alpha$. Negative coefficients are allowed: both covariances are multiplied by $\beta_{\alpha\alpha}^2$.

For either kind of summand $X$, $$\mathbb E\|X\|^3\le C\theta^3m_I^3.$$ For the rank-one term this follows from Gaussian moments of $\|g\|^2$; for the GOE term the Hilbert–Schmidt norm gives a sufficient bound. Fix spectral parameters $z_1,\ldots,z_b\in\mathbb H$, and a bounded smooth test $\Phi$ on the corresponding real and imaginary trace coordinates, with bounded derivatives. For any real symmetric matrix $A$, its resolvents satisfy $\|(A-z_\ell)^{-1}\|\le(\Im z_\ell)^{-1}$. Differentiating the resolvent in a real symmetric direction $\Delta$ gives, for $j=1,2,3$, a trace derivative bounded by $C_{z,j}m_I\|\Delta\|^j$. The chain rule therefore bounds the third directional derivative of the composed test by $$C_{z,\Phi}m_I^3\|\Delta\|^3.$$ This estimate holds at every intermediate real symmetric matrix, independently of eigenvalue gaps.

The Lindeberg replacement argument (see <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Chatterjee2006](#ref-Chatterjee2006)]) uses Taylor expansion to second order, which cancels the means and covariances in each replacement. Summing the third-order remainders gives total error $$C_{z,\Phi}m_I^6 r\theta^3
 =O\bigl(r^{-0.44+(3/2)\tau}\bigr)=o(1).$$ Finally $\sum_\alpha\beta_{\alpha\alpha}\mathcal W_\alpha$ has exactly the law $\sqrt{s_2}\,\mathcal W$, proving the lemma. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-sw-lindeberg-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-sw-lindeberg</summary>

<a id="eq:sw-trace-limit"></a>
*Completion of Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-insertion">20</a>.* The coefficient bounds were proved in <a href="#eq:insertion-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:insertion-coefficients">[eq:insertion-coefficients]</a>. Combine <a href="#eq:sw-finite-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-finite-matrix">[eq:sw-finite-matrix]</a> with the trace cut in Lemma <a href="#sw:trace-cut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sw:trace-cut">23</a> and the Gaussian replacement in Lemma <a href="#sw:lindeberg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sw:lindeberg">24</a>. They give a conditional comparison of bounded smooth tests of finitely many trace values with $\mathcal S_r$. Its error tends to zero for almost every $\mathcal E$, and is bounded, so bounded convergence removes the conditioning. The unconditional potential-switch identity from Proposition <a href="#prop:potential-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:potential-switch">19</a> therefore gives $$\label{eq:sw-trace-limit}
 \bigl(\Im\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_r-z_\ell)^{-1}\bigr)_{\ell=1}^b
   \ \Longrightarrow\
 \bigl(\Im m(z_\ell)\bigr)_{\ell=1}^b
 \qquad(z_1,\ldots,z_b\in\mathbb H).$$ Bounded smooth tests determine finite-dimensional weak convergence. In particular, switching invariance has only been used after averaging out the conditioning data.

We finish by explaining why these trace limits imply convergence of point measures, without assuming a uniform bound on their total number of points. Let $\nu^{(r)}$ be the eigenvalue counting measure of $H_r$, and define $$\sigma_r(\,d x)=\frac{\nu^{(r)}(\,d x)}{1+x^2}.$$ Its total mass is $\Im\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_r-i)^{-1}$, whose laws are tight by <a href="#eq:sw-trace-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-trace-limit">[eq:sw-trace-limit]</a>. Regarding $\sigma_r$ as a finite positive measure on the one-point compactification $\overline{\mathbb R}=\mathbb R\cup\{\infty\}$, its laws are therefore tight. For $z=e+i\eta\in\mathbb H$, the kernel $$k_z(x)=(1+x^2)\frac{\eta}{(x-e)^2+\eta^2}$$ extends continuously to $\overline{\mathbb R}$, with $k_z(\infty)=\eta$. Its integral against $\sigma_r$ is $\Im\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_r-z)^{-1}$. It follows that in any subsequential limit $\sigma$, the joint law of these integrals at a countable dense set of $z$’s is that of $\Im m(z)$. Continuity extends this conclusion to all $z$.

These integrals determine the de-weighted restriction of $\sigma$ to $\mathbb R$. Indeed, for $f\in C_c(\mathbb R)$, integrate the kernel against $\pi^{-1}f(e)\,d e$ and let $\eta\downarrow0$. On $\mathbb R$ the resulting kernel tends to $(1+x^2)f(x)$, by the Poisson approximate identity. The integrated kernels are bounded uniformly over $x\in\overline{\mathbb R}$ and $0<\eta\le1$: near the compact support this is the Poisson mass bound, and away from it the factor $1+x^2$ cancels the quadratic denominator. At infinity their value is $\eta\pi^{-1}\int f(e)\,d e$, which tends to zero. Dominated convergence is thus applicable to each finite measure $\sigma$, including any mass at infinity.

By Proposition <a href="#prop:marked-resolvent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:marked-resolvent">17</a>, applying the same inversion to $\Im m(z)$ gives $\int f\,\,d\nu$. Taking a countable determining family of compactly supported tests, the joint distribution of these integrals is therefore exactly the law of $\nu$. The map from $\sigma$ to its de-weighted restriction is continuous for local vague convergence: for a compactly supported $f$, the function $(1+x^2)f(x)$, extended by zero at infinity, is continuous on $\overline{\mathbb R}$. Every subsequential local vague limit of $\nu^{(r)}$ consequently has the law of $\nu$. This proves the point-process assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:dbm"></a>
### Identification by finite-matrix Dyson Brownian motion

Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-insertion">20</a> approximates every microscopic subsequential law by the eigenvalues of a finite diagonal matrix plus GOE noise. We now apply the fixed-energy theorem of Landon, Sosoe, and Yau <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[LSY2019](#ref-LSY2019)] to these finite matrices. Two details require attention: their limiting density must be normalized correctly, and the correlation-function conclusion must be converted into the Laplace-functional statement of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### The fixed-energy input

<a id="eq:dbm-regularity"></a>
<a id="eq:dbm-parameter-domain"></a>
<a id="eq:dbm-free-convolution"></a>
For a real diagonal matrix $V=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(v_1,\ldots,v_m)$, write $$m_V(\zeta)=\frac1m\sum_{j=1}^m\frac1{v_j-\zeta}.$$ For positive parameters $g,G$, we call $V$ $(g,G)$-regular if, for fixed positive constants $c,C,C_V$, $$\label{eq:dbm-regularity}
 c\le \Im m_V(e+i\eta)\le C
 \quad (|e|\le G,\ g\le\eta\le10),
 \qquad \|V\|\le m^{C_V}.$$ The parameters may depend on $m$, and satisfy, for a fixed $\delta>0$, $$\label{eq:dbm-parameter-domain}
 m^{\delta-1}\le g\le m^{-\delta},
 \qquad G\le m^{-\delta}.$$ Let $W_m$ be standard normalized GOE, with entry variances $1/m$ off the diagonal and $2/m$ on the diagonal. The deterministic comparison density for $V+\sqrt t\,W_m$ is defined by the free-convolution transform $$\label{eq:dbm-free-convolution}
 m_{\mathrm{fc},t}(\zeta)
   =m_V\bigl(\zeta+t\,m_{\mathrm{fc},t}(\zeta)\bigr),
 \qquad
 \rho_{\mathrm{fc},t}(e)
   =\frac1\pi\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}
          \Im m_{\mathrm{fc},t}(e+i\eta).$$ Here the solution with positive imaginary part is used in the upper half-plane.

We state the specialization of the fixed-energy theorem that we need. Its density convention is important. If $p_H^{(m)}$ denotes the symmetrized joint eigenvalue density of a random matrix $H$, normalized to have integral one, then $$p_H^{(a)}(y_1,\ldots,y_a)
   =\int_{\mathbb R^{m-a}}p_H^{(m)}(y_1,\ldots,y_m)
        \,d y_{a+1}\cdots\,d y_m$$ also has integral one. It is not the factorial moment density.

<a id="stmt-thm-dbm-input"></a>
<div id="thm:dbm-input" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:dbm-time-window"></a>
<a id="eq:dbm-marginal-test"></a>
**Theorem 25** (Fixed-energy DBM input). *Suppose a sequence of deterministic diagonal matrices $V$ of dimension $m\to\infty$ satisfies <a href="#eq:dbm-regularity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-regularity">[eq:dbm-regularity]</a> and <a href="#eq:dbm-parameter-domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-parameter-domain">[eq:dbm-parameter-domain]</a>, with fixed constants. Fix $\sigma>0$, and suppose $$\label{eq:dbm-time-window}
 g m^\sigma\le t\le m^{-\sigma}G^2.$$ Assume also that $\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t}(0)=1/\pi$. For every fixed positive integer $a$ and $O\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^a)$, the difference $$\begin{aligned}
 \label{eq:dbm-marginal-test}
 &\int_{\mathbb R^a}O(\boldsymbol\alpha)
   p_{V+\sqrt t\,W_m}^{(a)}
      \left(\frac{\pi\alpha_1}{m},\ldots,
            \frac{\pi\alpha_a}{m}\right)
       \,d\boldsymbol\alpha \notag\\
 &\hspace{12mm}-
 \int_{\mathbb R^a}O(\boldsymbol\alpha)
   p_{W_m}^{(a)}
      \left(\frac{\pi\alpha_1}{m},\ldots,
            \frac{\pi\alpha_a}{m}\right)
       \,d\boldsymbol\alpha
 \longrightarrow0.
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently, the expected sums of $O$ over ordered tuples of distinct points of the two eigenvalue processes, both rescaled by $m/\pi$, differ by $o(1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-dbm-input"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-dbm-input</summary>

*Proof.* The marginal statement is <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[LSY2019](#ref-LSY2019), Theorem 2.2], with Definition 2.1 and Equations (2.5)–(2.9) there, specialized to energy zero and equal densities $\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t}(0)=\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(0)=1/\pi$. The energy lies in the required interior window for any fixed interior fraction. That theorem gives a power-decaying bound for <a href="#eq:dbm-marginal-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-marginal-test">[eq:dbm-marginal-test]</a>. To pass to the last assertion, multiply each integral by $$\frac{(m)_a}{(m/\pi)^a},
 \qquad
 (m)_a=m(m-1)\cdots(m-a+1).$$ This is the Jacobian and ordered-tuple multiplicity for the unit-mass marginal convention, and it tends to $\pi^a$ for fixed $a$. ◻

</details>

Theorem <a href="#thm:dbm-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:dbm-input">25</a> applies to arbitrary deterministic real diagonal matrices satisfying its displayed hypotheses. The same specialization is recorded in <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[bu](#ref-bu), Theorem 6.1].

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Regularity and the density adjustment

<a id="eq:dbm-raw-matrix"></a>
<a id="eq:dbm-dimension"></a>
Fix a microscopic subsequential law $\nu$ supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:marked-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:marked-limits">10</a>. We use the parameters and conditional matrices of Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-insertion">20</a>: $$K=r^{1/100},\qquad T=r^\tau,\qquad
 0<\tau<\frac{\chi}{100000},\qquad
 I=\{j:|x_j|\le K\},\qquad m_I=|I|,$$ and $$\label{eq:dbm-raw-matrix}
 H_r=D_I+s_1\mathrm I+\sqrt{s_2}\,\mathcal W_r,
 \qquad |s_1|\le C T,\qquad cT\le s_2\le CT.$$ Conditional on $\mathcal E$, $\mathcal W_r$ is raw GOE of dimension $m_I$, with variances $1$ and $2$. All following almost-sure assertions concern the conditioning input $\mathcal E$ defined in Section <a href="#sec:insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:insertion">7</a>. In particular, the discrepancy bound of Lemma <a href="#lem:discrepancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:discrepancy">16</a> gives $$\label{eq:dbm-dimension}
 m_I=2\rho K+O(K^{1-\chi}),
 \qquad
 T=m_I^{100\tau+o(1)}.$$ Thus $m_I>0$ eventually. We may decrease $\chi$ so that $\chi<1/4$.

<a id="eq:dbm-normalized-matrix"></a>
Put $\alpha_*=\pi\rho$. Scaling <a href="#eq:dbm-raw-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-raw-matrix">[eq:dbm-raw-matrix]</a> by $\alpha_*/m_I$ gives the normalized GOE-addition model $$\label{eq:dbm-normalized-matrix}
 \frac{\alpha_*}{m_I}H_r
   \ \stackrel{\mathrm{law}}{=}\
       V_*+\sqrt{t_*}\,W_{m_I},
 \qquad
 V_*=\frac{\alpha_*}{m_I}(D_I+s_1\mathrm I),
 \qquad
 t_*=\frac{\alpha_*^2s_2}{m_I}.$$

The following verification adapts the calculation in <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[bu](#ref-bu), Section 6.2]. We give it in full for the random coefficients constructed here.

<a id="stmt-lem-density-adjustment"></a>
<div id="lem:density-adjustment" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26**. *For almost every realization of $\mathcal E$, the matrices $V_*$ are eventually regular with $$g=\frac{T^{1/2}}{m_I},\qquad G=m_I^{-1/8}.$$ They satisfy the hypotheses <a href="#eq:dbm-parameter-domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-parameter-domain">[eq:dbm-parameter-domain]</a> and <a href="#eq:dbm-time-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-time-window">[eq:dbm-time-window]</a> with $m=m_I$ and $\delta=\sigma=\tau$. Moreover, $$\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t_*}(0)\longrightarrow\frac1\pi.$$ If $c_r=\pi\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t_*}(0)$, then eventually $c_r>0$, and the data $c_rV_*$ and time $c_r^2t_*$ satisfy Theorem <a href="#thm:dbm-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:dbm-input">25</a>, including its exact density condition.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-density-adjustment"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-density-adjustment</summary>

<a id="eq:dbm-original-domain"></a>
*Proof.* First, $\|V_*\|$ is bounded by <a href="#eq:dbm-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-dimension">[eq:dbm-dimension]</a>, since $T=o(K)$. We verify a slightly enlarged regularity domain, $$|\Re\zeta|\le2G,\qquad g/2\le\Im\zeta\le20,$$ so that subsequent dilation by $c_r=1+o(1)$ will be harmless. The normalized resolvent trace is $$m_{V_*}(\zeta)=
   \alpha_*^{-1}\sum_{j\in I}
        \frac1{x_j-(m_I\zeta/\alpha_*-s_1)}.$$ Writing $e=m_I\Re\zeta/\alpha_*-s_1$ and $u=m_I\Im\zeta/\alpha_*$, the enlarged domain has $$\label{eq:dbm-original-domain}
 |e|=O(K^{7/8}+T)=o(K),\qquad
 cT^{1/2}\le u\le CK.$$ The lower height exceeds a fixed positive power of $K$. Proposition <a href="#prop:transform-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transform-regularity">18</a>, with a radius comparable to $K$ and any fixed exponent smaller than $50\tau$, therefore gives $$\sum_j\frac{u}{(x_j-e)^2+u^2}
       =\pi\rho+o(1)$$ uniformly in <a href="#eq:dbm-original-domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-original-domain">[eq:dbm-original-domain]</a>. For $u/K$ sufficiently small, the omitted terms with $|x_j|>K$ sum to $O(u/K)$, uniformly for $|e|=o(K)$, by the linear count bound from Lemma <a href="#lem:discrepancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:discrepancy">16</a>. Hence the retained sum has a positive lower bound. For $u/K$ bounded below, every retained point has $|x_j-e|\le2K$ eventually, so $$\sum_{j\in I}\frac{u}{(x_j-e)^2+u^2}
   \ge \frac{m_Iu}{4K^2+u^2}\ge c>0.$$ Its upper bound follows by positivity from the full sum. This proves regularity on the enlarged domain.

With $m=m_I$, the relevant powers are $$g=m^{-1+50\tau+o(1)},\qquad
 t_*=m^{-1+100\tau+o(1)},\qquad G=m^{-1/8}.$$ In particular, $$\frac{t_*}{g m^\tau}=m^{49\tau+o(1)}\longrightarrow\infty,
 \qquad
 \frac{t_*}{m^{-\tau}G^2}
     =m^{-3/4+101\tau+o(1)}\longrightarrow0.$$ The inequalities $m^{\tau-1}\le g\le m^{-\tau}$ and $G\le m^{-\tau}$ follow as well. Our bound on $\tau$ makes all these exponent inequalities strict.

<a id="eq:dbm-boundary-equation"></a>
It remains to determine the free-convolution density. At $\zeta=0$, Equation <a href="#eq:dbm-free-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-free-convolution">[eq:dbm-free-convolution]</a> becomes $$\label{eq:dbm-boundary-equation}
 \mathfrak m=F_r(\mathfrak m),\qquad
 F_r(\mathfrak m)=\alpha_*^{-1}\sum_{j\in I}
     \frac1{x_j-(\alpha_*s_2\mathfrak m-s_1)}.$$ For $|\mathfrak m-i|\le1/4$, the argument $z=\alpha_*s_2\mathfrak m-s_1$ has $\Im z\asymp T$ and $|z|=O(T)$. Propositions <a href="#prop:transform-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:transform-regularity">18</a> and <a href="#prop:marked-resolvent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:marked-resolvent">17</a> imply $$\mathop{\mathrm{PV}}\sum_j\frac1{x_j-z}=i\pi\rho+o(1)$$ uniformly in this disk, with the symmetric principal-value convention of Section <a href="#sec:resolvents" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:resolvents">6</a>. Furthermore, $$\mathop{\mathrm{PV}}\sum_{|x_j|>K}\frac1{x_j-z}
   =\mathop{\mathrm{PV}}\sum_{|x_j|>K}\frac1{x_j}
      +\sum_{|x_j|>K}\frac{z}{x_j(x_j-z)}
   =O(K^{-\chi}+T/K).$$ The first estimate follows by partial summation in Lemma <a href="#lem:discrepancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:discrepancy">16</a>; the second uses its linear count bound and $|z|=o(K)$. Thus $F_r(\mathfrak m)=i+o(1)$ uniformly on $|\mathfrak m-i|\le1/4$.

<a id="eq:dbm-uniqueness-identity"></a>
Rouché’s theorem applied to $\mathfrak m-F_r(\mathfrak m)$ and $\mathfrak m-i$ shows that <a href="#eq:dbm-boundary-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-boundary-equation">[eq:dbm-boundary-equation]</a> has exactly one zero, counted with multiplicity, in this disk for all large $r$. The zero $\mathfrak m_r$ is therefore simple, and $\mathfrak m_r\to i$. The implicit function theorem continues it to a solution near $\zeta=0$ of the full fixed-point equation, still with positive imaginary part. This solution is the physical branch. Indeed, every upper-half-plane solution $\mathfrak m$, at $\Im\zeta>0$, satisfies $$\label{eq:dbm-uniqueness-identity}
 \frac1{m_I}\sum_{j\in I}
   |(V_*)_{jj}-\zeta-t_*\mathfrak m|^{-2}
       =\frac{\Im \mathfrak m}{\Im\zeta+t_*\Im \mathfrak m}<\frac1{t_*}.$$ If two distinct such solutions existed, subtracting their equations and dividing by their difference would give $$1=\frac{t_*}{m_I}\sum_{j\in I}
  \frac1{((V_*)_{jj}-\zeta-t_*\mathfrak m_1)
          ((V_*)_{jj}-\zeta-t_*\mathfrak m_2)}.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#eq:dbm-uniqueness-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-uniqueness-identity">[eq:dbm-uniqueness-identity]</a> make the absolute value of the right-hand side strictly less than one. This contradiction proves uniqueness and identifies the analytic continuation. Its boundary value gives $\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t_*}(0)=\Im \mathfrak m_r/\pi\to1/\pi$.

Finally, simultaneous replacement of $V_*,t_*$ by $c_rV_*,c_r^2t_*$ dilates the free-convolution law by $c_r$. For example its transform is $c_r^{-1}m_{\mathrm{fc},t_*}(\zeta/c_r)$, as follows directly from <a href="#eq:dbm-free-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-free-convolution">[eq:dbm-free-convolution]</a>. Its density at zero is exactly $$c_r^{-1}\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t_*}(0)=\frac1\pi.$$ Since $c_r\to1$, the enlarged regularity domain proves <a href="#eq:dbm-regularity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-regularity">[eq:dbm-regularity]</a> for $c_rV_*$ with the original $g,G$. The strict power margins preserve <a href="#eq:dbm-time-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-time-window">[eq:dbm-time-window]</a> for $c_r^2t_*$, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

The conclusion holds for almost every realization of $\mathcal E$, which is enough for applying Theorem <a href="#thm:dbm-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:dbm-input">25</a> to a deterministic sequence. No assertion of switching invariance conditional on $\nu$ is used. The rescaled eigenvalues supplied by that theorem are, by <a href="#eq:dbm-normalized-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-normalized-matrix">[eq:dbm-normalized-matrix]</a>, precisely <a id="eq:dbm-final-coordinates"></a>$$\label{eq:dbm-final-coordinates}
 \frac{m_I}{\pi}\frac{c_r\alpha_*}{m_I}
       \lambda_j(H_r)
       =c_r\rho\,\lambda_j(H_r).$$ Define $c_r=1$ at any exceptional initial values where the preceding construction is not defined; these values have no effect on the limit.

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### The GOE limit and uniform count moments

Let $$\mathcal G_m=\sum_{j=1}^m
       \delta_{(m/\pi)\lambda_j(W_m)}.$$ These point processes converge vaguely in law, along all dimensions, to a common locally finite point process, which we denote by $\mathcal S_{\mathrm{GOE}}$. To check normalization directly, the eigenvalues of $\sqrt m\,W_m$ have joint density proportional to $$\exp\left(-\frac14\sum_j y_j^2\right)
       \prod_{i<j}|y_i-y_j|.$$ This follows from orthogonal diagonalization of the matrix density $\exp(-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}H^2/4)$; the change in an off-diagonal coordinate under an infinitesimal basis rotation is the corresponding eigenvalue difference, yielding the Vandermonde Jacobian. Theorem 1 of Valkó and Virág <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[ValkoVirag2009](#ref-ValkoVirag2009)], for $\beta=1$ and center zero, gives vague convergence after multiplying these eigenvalues by $2\sqrt m$. Its bulk condition $m^{1/6}(2\sqrt m-0)\to\infty$ holds. Dividing the resulting coordinates by $2\pi$ gives $\mathcal G_m$, as claimed.

Weak convergence alone does not justify termwise expectations of correlation expansions. The following bound makes the finite inclusion–exclusion brackets converge uniformly for the reference ensemble.

<a id="stmt-lem-goe-counts"></a>
<div id="lem:goe-counts" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27**. *For every compact interval $J\subset\mathbb R$ and every $b>0$, $$\sup_{m\ge1}\mathbb E\exp\bigl(b\,\mathcal G_m(J)\bigr)<\infty.$$*

</div>

The proof is given in Appendix <a href="#app:goe-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:goe-counts">9</a>. It uses Gaussian decimation to compare the GOE count with a determinantal count, followed by a uniform bound on its projection kernel.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### From correlation functions to Laplace functionals

<a id="eq:dbm-insertion-limit"></a>
We use the finite Bonferroni comparison in <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[bu](#ref-bu), Section 6.4] to pass from conditional DBM convergence to the unconditional point-process law. Let $\nu^{(r)}=\sum_{j=1}^{m_I}\delta_{\lambda_j(H_r)}$ denote the raw eigenvalue measure in <a href="#eq:dbm-raw-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-raw-matrix">[eq:dbm-raw-matrix]</a>. Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-insertion">20</a> asserts $$\label{eq:dbm-insertion-limit}
 \nu^{(r)}\ \Longrightarrow\ \nu$$ in local vague topology, unconditionally. We will identify the law of $\rho$-dilated $\nu$ by first applying DBM conditional on $\mathcal E$.

<a id="eq:dbm-bonferroni"></a>
Fix a nonnegative $h\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$, and put $f=1-e^{-h}$, so $0\le f\le1$. For a finite point measure $\zeta=\sum_i\delta_{y_i}$, define $$A_0(\zeta)=1,\qquad
 A_a(\zeta)=\frac1{a!}
    \sum_{i_1,\ldots,i_a\ {\rm distinct}}
        \prod_{j=1}^a f(y_{i_j})\quad(a\ge1),
 \qquad
 B_\ell(\zeta)=\sum_{a=0}^{\ell}(-1)^aA_a(\zeta).$$ The finite inclusion-exclusion inequalities give $$\label{eq:dbm-bonferroni}
 B_{2\ell+1}(\zeta)
   \le \exp\left(-\int h\,d\zeta\right)
   \le B_{2\ell}(\zeta).$$ One can see these inequalities by taking independent Bernoulli variables of success probabilities $f(y_i)$: the middle quantity is their probability of no successes, and the displayed sums are the Bonferroni bounds for that event.

<a id="eq:dbm-bracket-width"></a>
<a id="eq:dbm-conditional-laplace"></a>
Conditional on almost every realization of $\mathcal E$, let $\zeta_r$ have the point coordinates $c_r\rho\,\lambda_j(H_r)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:density-adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:density-adjustment">26</a> and Theorem <a href="#thm:dbm-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:dbm-input">25</a>, applied with the test $\prod_{j=1}^a f(\alpha_j)$, imply for each fixed $\ell$ that $$\mathbb E[B_\ell(\zeta_r)\mid\mathcal E]
       -\mathbb EB_\ell(\mathcal G_{m_I})\longrightarrow0.$$ The expectation in the second term is over a reference GOE matrix of the now deterministic conditional dimension $m_I$. To close the brackets uniformly in that dimension, choose a compact interval $J$ containing $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}h$. Then $$A_a(\mathcal G_m)\le
     \binom{\mathcal G_m(J)}a
     \le 2^{-a}3^{\mathcal G_m(J)}.$$ The last inequality follows from $\sum_{a\ge0}2^a\binom{N}{a}=3^N$. Lemma <a href="#lem:goe-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:goe-counts">27</a> consequently gives $$\label{eq:dbm-bracket-width}
 \sup_m\mathbb E\bigl[B_{2\ell}(\mathcal G_m)
                 -B_{2\ell+1}(\mathcal G_m)\bigr]
       \le C_J2^{-(2\ell+1)}.$$ Using <a href="#eq:dbm-bonferroni" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-bonferroni">[eq:dbm-bonferroni]</a> for both models, first sending $r\to\infty$ at fixed $\ell$, and then sending $\ell\to\infty$, proves $$\label{eq:dbm-conditional-laplace}
 \mathbb E\left[\exp\left(-\int h\,d\zeta_r\right)\,\middle|\,\mathcal E\right]
   \longrightarrow
 \mathbb E\exp\left(-\int h\,d\mathcal S_{\mathrm{GOE}}\right).$$ Here we also used the vague GOE limit and $m_I\to\infty$. Only the reference count moments were needed in <a href="#eq:dbm-bracket-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-bracket-width">[eq:dbm-bracket-width]</a>; no uniform moment bound for the deformed model has been assumed. Since both sides of the conditional Laplace comparison lie in $[0,1]$, bounded convergence now removes the conditioning in <a href="#eq:dbm-conditional-laplace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-conditional-laplace">[eq:dbm-conditional-laplace]</a>.

<a id="eq:dbm-limit-identification"></a>
For $c>0$, let $\mathcal D_c\zeta$ denote the point measure obtained by multiplying all positions of $\zeta$ by $c$. The map $(c,\zeta)\mapsto\mathcal D_c\zeta$ is continuous when $c$ tends to a positive number and $\zeta$ converges locally vaguely. Indeed, the inverse images of a fixed compact support remain in a common compact interval, and the corresponding test functions converge uniformly there; vague convergence also bounds the masses on a slightly larger compact interval. By Lemma <a href="#lem:density-adjustment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:density-adjustment">26</a>, $c_r\to1$ almost surely. Combining this with <a href="#eq:dbm-insertion-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-insertion-limit">[eq:dbm-insertion-limit]</a> and the usual joint convergence with a constant yields $$\zeta_r=\mathcal D_{c_r\rho}\nu^{(r)}
       \ \Longrightarrow\ \mathcal D_\rho\nu.$$ The Laplace functional of a compactly supported continuous nonnegative test is bounded and continuous in local vague topology. Thus <a href="#eq:dbm-conditional-laplace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-conditional-laplace">[eq:dbm-conditional-laplace]</a>, after averaging, identifies $$\label{eq:dbm-limit-identification}
 \mathbb E\exp\left(-\int h\,d\mathcal D_\rho\nu\right)
   =\mathbb E\exp\left(-\int h\,d\mathcal S_{\mathrm{GOE}}\right)
 \qquad (0\le h\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)).$$ For any $0\le h\in C_c(\mathbb R)$, convolution with a nonnegative smooth approximate identity gives nonnegative smooth functions converging uniformly to $h$, all supported in one fixed compact interval. Both limiting point measures have finitely many points there almost surely. The corresponding Laplace variables converge pointwise and are bounded by one, so bounded convergence extends <a href="#eq:dbm-limit-identification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-limit-identification">[eq:dbm-limit-identification]</a> to every such $h$.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-goe-counts"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-goe-counts</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Choose the fixed compact bulk interval $J$ with $I_{d,\kappa}\subset\operatorname{int}J$. The finitely many smallness choices in Proposition <a href="#prop:tree-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tree-stability">2</a> and the subsequent arguments give one positive number $w_0(d,\kappa)$. Fix any $0<w<w_0(d,\kappa)$ and $E\in I_{d,\kappa}$. Corollary <a href="#cor:tree-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:tree-density">4</a> gives a continuous density on an open neighborhood of $I_{d,\kappa}$, strictly positive there; write $\rho=\rho_{d,w}(E)>0$.

Start with any sequence of admissible graph sizes tending to infinity. Proposition <a href="#prop:marked-limits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:marked-limits">10</a> supplies a further subsequence on which the unscaled microscopic measures $\nu_n$ converge in law to a process $\nu$ of the form used above. The graph point measure in the theorem is $\mathcal D_\rho\nu_n$. Bounded continuity of its Laplace functional and <a href="#eq:dbm-limit-identification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-limit-identification">[eq:dbm-limit-identification]</a> show that its Laplace expectation, along this further subsequence, tends to the Laplace expectation of $\mathcal S_{\mathrm{GOE}}$. The reference GOE expectations at dimension $n$ tend to the same value, along all sizes. Every original subsequence therefore has a further subsequence along which their difference tends to zero, which proves the required full-sequence limit. All spectral estimates and negligible exclusions used to construct the subsequential law were events in the original simple-graph model; no conditioning on connectedness, bipartiteness, or any additional graph property enters the conclusion. The constants in estimates that use spectral averaging may depend on this fixed positive $w$. The argument therefore proves the assertion for every fixed $w\in(0,w_0(d,\kappa))$, with the limit in graph size taken afterward. ◻

</details>

<a id="app:goe-counts"></a>
### Exponential count moments for GOE

We prove the dimension-uniform count bound used in Section <a href="#sec:dbm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:dbm">8</a> to pass from correlation functions to Laplace functionals. As there, $W_m$ is normalized GOE and $$\mathcal G_m=\sum_{j=1}^m
       \delta_{(m/\pi)\lambda_j(W_m)}.$$ The argument is independent of the Anderson model: Gaussian decimation compares GOE counts with those of a determinantal process, whose projection kernel gives the required exponential moments.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-goe-counts-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-goe-counts</summary>

*Proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:goe-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:goe-counts">27</a>.* Fix a compact interval $J\subset\mathbb R$ and a number $b>0$. We use the Gaussian decimation identity of Forrester and Rains <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[ForresterRains2001](#ref-ForresterRains2001), arXiv version 2, Theorem 5.2 and Equation (5.9)] and the count estimate in <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[bu](#ref-bu), Lemma 6.3]. We reproduce the identity in our normalization before proving the dimension-uniform bound. Write $\mathrm O_m$ for the decreasingly ordered points with density proportional to $$\Delta(y)\prod_{i=1}^m b_0(y_i),\qquad
 b_0(y)=e^{-y^2/2},\qquad
 \Delta(y)=\prod_{i<j}(y_i-y_j).$$ They have the law of the eigenvalues of $\sqrt m\,W_m/\sqrt2$. Thus the count $\mathcal G_m(J)$ is the $\mathrm O_m$ count in $$J_m=\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2m}}J,
 \qquad |J_m|=O(m^{-1/2}).$$

<a id="eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial"></a>
Superpose independent $\mathrm O_m$ and $\mathrm O_{m+1}$, and order the resulting points as $z_1>\cdots>z_{2m+1}$. Apart from a constant and the common Gaussian factor, their density is the polynomial $$P(z)=\sum_{\substack{S\subset\{1,\ldots,2m+1\}\\|S|=m}}
          \Delta(z_S)\Delta(z_{S^c}),$$ where each sublist is in the inherited order. The parity–Vandermonde factorization of <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[ForresterRains2001](#ref-ForresterRains2001), arXiv version 2, Lemma 4.1 and Equation (4.2)] is $$\label{eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial}
 P(z)=c_m\Delta(z_1,z_3,\ldots,z_{2m+1})
             \Delta(z_2,z_4,\ldots,z_{2m}),\qquad c_m>0.$$ If $z_i=z_j$ with $i,j$ of the same parity, terms having both indices in one sublist vanish. The other terms cancel in pairs under exchanging membership of $i,j$: the sign ratio is $(-1)^{j-i-1}=-1$, since each intervening index belongs to exactly one sublist. Hence every factor on the right divides $P$. Both sides have degree $m^2$, so the quotient is constant. It is positive on strictly decreasing $z$, proving <a href="#eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial">[eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial]</a>.

<a id="eq:dbm-even-density"></a>
Fix the even points $y_i=z_{2i}$. The odd points interlace them, one in each of $$(y_1,\infty),\ (y_2,y_1),\ldots,(y_m,y_{m-1}),\
 (-\infty,y_m).$$ To integrate their Vandermonde times their Gaussian factors, use the monic polynomials $$p_j(x)=(-1)^j\frac{b_0^{(j)}(x)}{b_0(x)},
 \qquad 0\le j\le m.$$ Writing the Vandermonde as the determinant of these polynomials reduces the integral, by multilinearity, to a determinant of integrals over the displayed intervals. Replace each row by the sum of it and all preceding rows. For column $j\ge1$, the first $m$ rows become $$\int_{y_i}^\infty p_j(x)b_0(x)\,d x
       =p_{j-1}(y_i)b_0(y_i),$$ and the last row is zero. In column zero the last row is $\int_\mathbb Rb_0$. Expansion along that last row leaves a Vandermonde in the $y_i$ times $\prod_i b_0(y_i)$. More explicitly, the ascending-degree polynomial columns give signs $(-1)^{m(m+1)/2}$ for the odd-point Vandermonde and $(-1)^{m(m-1)/2}$ for the even-point Vandermonde, while expansion along the last row contributes $(-1)^m$. Their product is one. Thus, if $x_0>y_1>x_1>\cdots>y_m>x_m$, the integral just evaluated is exactly $$\int_{\text{interlacing}}
        \Delta(x_0,\ldots,x_m)\prod_{i=0}^m b_0(x_i)
                \,d x_0\cdots\,d x_m
     =\sqrt{2\pi}\,\Delta(y)\prod_{i=1}^m b_0(y_i).$$ Together with the even-point factors already present in <a href="#eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial">[eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial]</a>, this proves that the even points have density proportional to $$\label{eq:dbm-even-density}
 \Delta(y)^2\prod_{i=1}^m e^{-y_i^2}.$$

Let $\psi_j(x)$ be the degree-$j$ orthonormal polynomial for the weight $e^{-x^2}$, multiplied by $e^{-x^2/2}$. The kernel $$K_m(x,y)=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\psi_j(x)\psi_j(y)$$ is the rank-$m$ orthogonal projection in $L^2(\mathbb R)$ onto their span. The symmetric density in <a href="#eq:dbm-even-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-even-density">[eq:dbm-even-density]</a> is $(m!)^{-1}\det(K_m(y_i,y_j))_{i,j=1}^m$. Indeed the determinant is a squared weighted Vandermonde, and orthonormality gives its integral $m!$. More generally its order-$a$ factorial moment density is $\det(K_m(y_i,y_j))_{i,j=1}^a$. For completeness, expand a size-$a$ determinant by Cauchy–Binet as the sum of squared minors indexed by $a$ basis functions. Integrating its last variable uses their orthogonality, and each minor of size $a-1$ occurs $m-a+1$ times. Iterating this identity proves the factorial density formula with its stated normalization.

We need a bound uniform in both $m$ and location. The operator $-\partial_x^2+x^2$ has eigenvalue $2j+1$ on $\psi_j$. This follows by conjugating it by $e^{-x^2/2}$: on the polynomial factor it acts as $-p''+2xp'+p$, preserves successive degree spaces, is symmetric for the Gaussian inner product, and has leading coefficient multiplier $2j+1$. Consequently every $f$ in the range of $K_m$ satisfies $$\|f'\|_2^2\le(2m-1)\|f\|_2^2.$$ The elementary bound $|f(x)|^2\le2\|f'\|_2\|f\|_2$, obtained by integrating the derivative of $|f|^2$, gives $$K_m(x,x)
   =\sup_{\substack{f\in\operatorname{Ran}K_m\\\|f\|_2=1}}
       |f(x)|^2
   \le2\sqrt{2m}.$$ If $N_m^{\mathrm{even}}$ is the number of even points in $J_m$, the Gram determinant inequality yields, for every $a\ge0$, $$\mathbb E(N_m^{\mathrm{even}})_a
   \le \bigl(2\sqrt{2m}\,|J_m|\bigr)^a\le C_J^a.$$ For $a>m$ the left side is zero. Hence, for every $v>0$, $$\mathbb Ee^{vN_m^{\mathrm{even}}}
   =\sum_{a=0}^m
       \frac{(e^v-1)^a}{a!}\mathbb E(N_m^{\mathrm{even}})_a
   \le\exp\bigl(C_J(e^v-1)\bigr).$$ In any interval the total superposition count is at most twice its even count plus one. The original $\mathrm O_m$ count is no greater than the superposition count. Thus $$\mathbb Ee^{b\mathcal G_m(J)}
   \le e^b\,\mathbb Ee^{2bN_m^{\mathrm{even}}},$$ which proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The upper switch attaches a sampled cavity at $u$ to the internal vertex $p$, retaining a reversible stub mark. The open circles mark the selected outgoing stubs at $u$ before the switch and $t$ after it. The lower re-pairing is used only to estimate Green entries between the two endpoints of a cut edge. After both displayed edges are removed the graph is unchanged, while $u$ and $v$ belong to different sampled edge groups. The drawings specify stub pairings; they assert no independence of neighboring Green functions.",
      "figure_id": "fig:local-switch",
      "pdf_page": 15,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:local-switch",
          "exact_quote": "The upper switch attaches a sampled cavity at $u$ to the\ninternal vertex $p$, retaining a reversible stub mark.  The open circles\nmark the selected outgoing stubs at $u$ before the switch and $t$ after it.\nThe lower re-pairing is used only ",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=.92,vertex/.style={circle,fill,inner sep=1.5pt},\n                    every node/.style={font=\\small},>=Stealth]\n \\node at (1.4,1.7) {boundary switch};\n \\node[vertex,label=left:$p$] (p1) at (0,1) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$t$] (t1) at (2,1) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=left:$u$] (u1) at (0,0) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$v$] (v1) at (2,0) {};\n \\draw[thick] (p1)--(t1) (u1)--(v1);\n \\node[circle,draw=magenta!70!black,fill=white,line width=1pt,\n       inner sep=2pt] at (.4,0) {};\n \\draw[->] (2.5,.5)--(3.3,.5);\n \\node[vertex,label=left:$p$] (p2) at (3.8,1) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$t$] (t2) at (5.8,1) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=left:$u$] (u2) at (3.8,0) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$v$] (v2) at (5.8,0) {};\n \\draw[thick,blue!65!black] (p2)--(u2) (t2)--(v2);\n \\node[circle,draw=magenta!70!black,fill=white,line width=1pt,\n       inner sep=2pt] at (5.8,.75) {};\n \\node[align=center] at (4.8,-.65) {far mark moves\\ from $u$ to $t$};\n \\begin{scope}[yshift=-3.3cm]\n \\node at (1.4,1.7) {re-pairing before two cuts};\n \\node[vertex,label=left:$u$] (u3) at (0,1) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$v$] (v3) at (2,1) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=left:$a$] (a3) at (0,0) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$b$] (b3) at (2,0) {};\n \\draw[thick,dashed] (u3)--(v3) (a3)--(b3);\n \\draw[->] (2.5,.5)--(3.3,.5);\n \\node[vertex,label=left:$u$] (u4) at (3.8,1) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$v$] (v4) at (5.8,1) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=left:$a$] (a4) at (3.8,0) {};\n \\node[vertex,label=right:$b$] (b4) at (5.8,0) {};\n \\draw[thick,dashed,blue!65!black] (u4)--(b4) (a4)--(v4);\n \\node[align=center] at (4.8,-.65) {delete both dashed edges:\\ the remaining graph is identical};\n \\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two schematic graph rewiring diagrams:\n1. Top diagram titled \"boundary switch\":\n   - Left configuration: Two horizontal solid edges. Upper edge connects vertex $p$ (solid black dot) to vertex $t$ (solid black dot). Lower edge connects vertex $u$ (open circle) to vertex $v$ (solid black dot).\n   - An arrow points from the left configuration to the right configuration.\n   - Right configuration: Two vertical solid edges. Left edge connects vertex $p$ (solid black dot) to vertex $u$ (solid black dot). Right edge connects vertex $t$ (open circle) to vertex $v$ (solid black dot). A curved purple arrow arcs from near vertex $u$ to vertex $t$, with text below stating \"far mark moves from $u$ to $t$\".\n2. Bottom diagram titled \"re-pairing before two cuts\":\n   - Left configuration: Two parallel horizontal dashed edges. Upper dashed edge connects vertex $u$ (solid black dot) to vertex $v$ (solid black dot). Lower dashed edge connects vertex $a$ (solid black dot) to vertex $b$ (solid black dot).\n   - An arrow points from the left configuration to the right configuration.\n   - Right configuration: Two crossing diagonal dashed edges. One connects vertex $u$ to vertex $b$, and the other connects vertex $a$ to vertex $v$.\n   - Text below reads: \"delete both dashed edges: the remaining graph is identical\"."
    }
  ],
